 35. Which dependency is removed when converting the Results table to next normal form?
     Results j.=j B,Õ m%u;lrKhg m;alrk úg my; l=uk mrdh;a;;dj (dependency) bj;a fõ o?
    1) foreign key dependency
       wd.ka;=l h;=re (foreign key) mrdh;a;;dj
    2) fully functional dependency of non-key attributes on the primary key
       h;=re fkdjk Wm,els" m%d:ñl h;=r u; mQ¾K ld¾hnoaO (full functional) mrdh;a;;dj
    3) multivalued dependency
       nyq w.h (multivalued) mrdh;a;;dj
    4) partial dependencies of non-key attributes on the primary key
       h;=re fkdjk Wm,els" m%d:ñl h;=r u; w¾O (partial) mrdh;a;;dj
    5) transitive dependency of non-key attributes
       h;=re fkdjk Wm,elsj, ixl%dka;s (transitive) mrdh;a;;dj

    Answer : 4 )

    Important concepts / jeo.;a ixl,am

    •   Foreign key dependency / wd.ka;=l h;=re mrdhla;;d
        Refers to a relationship between two tables based on foreign keys.
        wd.ka;=l h;=re u; mokï jQ j.= folla w;r iïnkaOhla .ek i|yka lrhs'

    •   Fully functional dependency / mq¾K ld¾hnoaO mrdhla;;d
        A non-key attribute depends on the entire primary key.
        h;=re fkdjk Wm,laIKhla iïmQ¾K m%d:ñl h;=r u; r|d mj;S'

    •   Multivalued dependency / nyqw.hka iys; mrdhla;;d
        Occurs when a non-key attribute depends on part of the primary key, but not the whole key.
        h;=re fkdjk Wm,laIKhla m%d:ñl h;=f¾ fldgila u; rojd mj;sk úg isÿ, iïmq¾K h;=r u;
        fkdfõ'

    •   Partial dependency / w¾O mrdhla;;d
        Occurs when a non-key attribute is dependent only on part of a composite key, rather than the
        entire key. Removing partial dependencies brings the table from 1NF to 2NF.
        h;=re fkdjk Wm,laIKhla iïmQ¾K h;=rg jvd ixhqla; h;=rl fldgila u; muKla r|d mj;sk
        úg isÿ fõ' w¾O mrdh;a;;d bj;a lsÍu j.=j 1NF isg 2NF olajd f.k ths'

    •   Transitive dependency / ixl%dka;s mrdhla;;d
        Occurs when a non-key attribute depends on another non-key attribute. Removing transitive
        dependencies brings the table to 3NF.
        h;=re fkdjk Wm,laIKhla fjk;a h;=re fkdjk Wm,laIKhla u; r|d mj;sk úg isÿ fõ'
        ixl%dka;s mrdh;a;;d bj;a lsÍu j.=j 3NF fj; f.k ths'

    Now, let's review each option.
    oeka" tla tla úl,amh iudf,dapkh lruq'

    1) Foreign key dependency
       wd.ka;=l h;=re mrdhla;;d
       This doesn't relate to the normal forms being discussed here. Foreign keys are used for
       relationships between tables, not for normalization. Thus, this statement is incorrect.
       fuh fuys idlÉPd flfrk m%u:lrK wjia:dj,g wod< fkdfõ' wd.ka;=l h;=re Ndú;d lrkafka
       j.= w;r iïnkaO;d i|yd ñi m%u:lrKh i|yd fkdfõ' fï wkqj" fuu m%ldYh idjoH fõ'

    2) Fully functional dependency of non-key attributes on the primary key
       h;=re fkdjk Wm,els" m%d:ñl h;=r u; mQ¾K ld¾hnoaO (full functional) mrdh;a;;dj
       This is actually required for higher normal forms, not removed. Thus, this statement is incorrect.
       fuh we;a; jYfhkau wjYH jkafka by< m%u:lrK wjia:djla i|yd ñi bj;a fkdflf¾' fï
       wkqj" fuu m%ldYh idjoH fõ'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                  -72-                                    #ictfromabc

    3) Multivalued dependency
       nyq w.h (multivalued) mrdh;a;;dj
       This is not relevant to the Results table because the table doesn’t exhibit multivalued dependency.
       Thus, this statement is incorrect.
       j.=j nyq w.hka iys; mrdh;a;;djhla fkdfmkajk neúka fuh Results j.=jg wod< fkdfõ'
       fï wkqj" fuu m%ldYh idjoH fõ'

    4) Partial dependencies of non-key attributes on the primary key
       h;=re fkdjk Wm,els" m%d:ñl h;=r u; w¾O (partial) mrdh;a;;dj
       When converting from 1NF to 2NF, partial dependencies are removed. In the Results table,
       attributes like NIC and FirstName are partially dependent on only StudentNo (part of the
       composite key). Therefore, this statement is correct.
       1NF isg 2NF olajd mßj¾;kh lsÍfï§" w¾O mrdh;a;;d bj;a lrkq ,efí' Results j.=fõ"
       NIC iy FirstName jeks Wm,laIKh w¾O jYfhka r|d mj;skafka StudentNo ^ixhqla; h;=f¾
       fldgila& u; muKs' tA ksid fuu m%ldYh ksrjoH fõ'

    5) Transitive dependency of non-key attributes
       h;=re fkdjk Wm,elsj, ixl%dka;s (transitive) mrdh;a;;dj
       Transitive dependencies are removed when moving from 2NF to 3NF. However, the Results
       table currently shows partial dependencies, not transitive dependencies. Thus, this statement is
       incorrect.
       2NF isg 3NF olajd .uka lrk úg ixl%dka;s mrdh;a;;d bj;a lrkq ,efí' flfia fj;;a" Results
       j.=j oekg fmkakqï lrkafka ixl%dka;s mrdh;a;;d fkdj w¾O mrdh;a;;d h' fï wkqj" fuu
       m%ldYh idjoH fõ'

    The correct answer is (4) partial dependencies of non-key attributes on the primary key.
    ksjerÈ ms<s;=r jkafka (4) h;=re fkdjk Wm,els" m%d:ñl h;=r u; w¾O mrdh;a;;djhhs'

 36. Following are the steps involved in creating an Entity Relationship (ER) Diagram:
     N+;d¾: iïnkaO;d (ER) rE igykla ks¾udKh lsÍug wod< mshjr my; oelafõ'

        iEu iïnkaO;djhlgu ……E…… o tla lrkak'

    by; A, B, C, D iy E ysia;eka i|yd ksjerÈ f;dard.ekSï olajkafka my; ljrla o?

    Answer : 3 )
    In an Entity-Relationship (ER) diagram, the core elements include entities, attributes, relationships, and
    cardinality. These concepts help to visually represent the data structure and relationships in a database
    system.
    N+;d¾;-iïnkaO;d ^ER& igykl" uQ,sl uQ,dx.j,g N+;d¾;" Wm,laIK" iïnkaO;d iy .KkSh;dj
    we;=<;a fõ' fuu ixl,am o;a; iuqod moaO;shl o;a; jHqyh iy iïnkaO;d oDYH f,i ksrEmKh
    lsÍug WmldÍ fõ'
    Entities / N+;d¾;
    Entities represent real-world objects or concepts that have a distinct existence in the system. They are
    typically things or objects that need to be stored in a database.
    N+;d¾; u.ska moaO;sh ;=< meyeÈ,s meje;aula we;s ienE f,dalfha jia;+ka fyda ixl,am ksfhdackh
    lrhs' tAjd idudkHfhka o;a; .nvdjl .nvd l< hq;= foaj,a fyda jia;= fõ'
    Attributes / Wm,els
    Attributes are the properties or characteristics of an entity. They define the details or qualities of an entity
    that need to be stored in the database.
    Wm,els hkq jia;=jl .=Kdx. fyda ,laIK fõ' o;a; .nvdfõ .nvd l< hq;= N+;d¾;hl úia;r fyda
    .=Kdx. Tjqka ks¾jpkh lrhs'
    Relationships / iïnkaO;d
    Relationships represent the associations or interactions between two or more entities. They show how
    entities are related to each other.
    iïnkaO;d u.ska N+;d¾; folla fyda jeä .Kkla w;r we;s iïnkaOlï fyda wka;¾l%shd ksfhdackh
    lrhs' N+;d¾; tlsfkl iïnkaO jk wdldrh tAjd fmkajhs'
    Cardinality / .KkSh;dj
    Cardinality defines the numerical relationship between entities. It specifies how many instances of one
    entity can be associated with instances of another entity.
    .KkSh;dj N+;d¾; w;r ixLHd;aul iïnkaO;djh ks¾jpkh lrhs' th tla N+;d¾;hl wjia:d lShla
    ;j;a N+;d¾;hl wjia:d iuÕ iïnkaO l< yelso hkak i|yka lrhs'
    The steps to create this Entity-Relationship (ER) diagram are as follows.
    fuu N+;d¾; iïnkaO;d igykla ks¾udKh lsÍfï mshjr my; mßÈ fõ'
    1 - The first step in creating an ER diagram is to determine the entities (real-world objects or concepts)
        that will be represented in the diagram.
        ER igykla ks¾udKh lsÍfï m<uq mshjr jkafka igyfkys ksrEmKh jk N+;d¾; ^ienE f,dal
        jia;= fyda ixl,am& ;SrKh lsÍuhs'
    2 - Each entity is characterized by attributes (details or properties of the entity). The entities are the core
        components of an ER diagram, so each attribute is added to an entity.
        iEu N+;d¾;hlau Wm,els j,ska ix,laIs; fõ ^N+;d¾;fha úia;r fyda .=Kdx.&' N+;d¾; hkq ER
        rEm igykl uQ,sl fldgia fõ" tneúka tla tla Wm,elsh" N+;d¾;hlg tl;= fõ'
    3 - After defining the entities, the next step is to include the relationships between them, which represent
        how entities interact with each other.
        N+;d¾; ks¾jpkh lsÍfuka miq" B<Õ mshjr jkafka tAjd w;r iïnkaO;d we;=<;a lsÍuhs" th
        N+;d¾; tlsfkld iuÕ wka;¾ l%shd lrk wdldrh ksfhdackh lrhs'
    4 - Cardinality defines the number of occurrences of one entity that can be associated with the number
        of occurrences of another entity in a relationship.
        .KkSh;dj u.ska iïnkaO;djhl" tla N+;d¾;hl isÿùï .Kk iuÕ iïnkaO l< yels ;j;a
        N+;d¾;hl isÿùï .Kk ks¾jpkh lrhs'
    Accordingly, the answers are given below.
    tA wkqj my; wdldrhg ms<s;=re ,efí'

    A – entities / N+;d¾;                      B – attributes / Wm,els                     C – entity / N+;d¾;h
    D – relationships / iïnkaO;d               E – cardinality / .KkSh;dj
    So, the correct answer number is 3.
    tuksid, ksjerÈ ms<s;=re wxlh 3 fõ'

 37. Which of the following can be modelled with an Extended Entity Relationship diagram?
     úia;D; N+;d¾: iïnkaO;d (Extended Entity Relationship) rE igyklska my; ljrla ksrEmKh l<
     yels o?
    A - subclasses of an entity N+;d¾:hl Wmmka;s
    B - inheritance of attributes Wm,els Wreuùu
    C - specialization of entities N+;d¾:j, úfYaIdhkh

    1) A only.           2) B only.           3) C only.           4) A and C only.     5) All of the above.
       A muKs.              B muKs.              C muKs.              A yd C muKs.         by; ish,a,u.

    Answer : 5 )

    An Enhanced Entity-Relationship (EER) diagram is a visual representation of a database schema that
    extends the traditional Entity-Relationship (ER) model to capture more complex relationships and data
    structures. It's a valuable tool for database design, especially when dealing with intricate systems.
    úia;D; N+;d¾;-iïnkaO;d ^EER& igykla hkq jvd;a ixlS¾K iïnkaO;d iy o;a; jHqyhka .%yKh
    lr .ekSu i|yd iïm%odhsl N+;d¾;-iïnkaO;d ^ER& wdlD;sh È.= lrk o;a; iuqod l%uhl oDYH
    ksrEmKhls' th úfYaIfhka ixlS¾K moaO;s iuÕ lghq;= lrk úg" o;a; iuqod ks¾udKh i|yd jákd
    fuj,uls'

    Subclasses of an entity / N+;d¾:hl Wmmka;s
    Subclasses represent a more specific version of an entity. For example, if you have an entity called Person,
    subclasses could be Student and Teacher. These subclasses inherit attributes from the parent entity Person
    (e.g., name, age), but may also have their own unique attributes (e.g., grade for Student, subject for
    Teacher). In EER subclasses allow more detailed representation of entities with variations in behavior or
    characteristics.
    Wmmka;s hkq N+;d¾;hl jvd;a ksYaÑ; wkqjdohls' WodyrKhla f,i" Tng mqoa.,hd kñka N+;d¾;hla
    ;sfí kï" Wmmka;s YsIH iy .=re úh yel' fuu Wmmka;sj,g m%Odk N+;d¾;h jk mqoa.,hdf.ka
    .=Kdx. ^Wod' ku" jhi& Wreu fõ" kuq;a Tjqkagu wkkH .=Kdx. o ;sìh yelsh ^Wod( YsIH i|yd
    fY%aKsh" .=rejrhd i|yd úIh&' EER Wmmka;sj, yeisÍï fyda ,laIKj, fjkialï iys; N+;d¾;
    jvd;a iúia;rd;aulj ksrEmKh lsÍug bv i,ihs'

    Inheritance of attributes / Wm,els Wreuùu
    Inheritance refers to the concept where a subclass inherits attributes from its parent entity. For example, a
    Teacher (subclass) inherits common attributes like name and age from the Person (parent entity) but may
    also have additional attributes like subject. In EER inheritance allows for sharing of common attributes
    across related entities, reducing redundancy.
    Wreuh hkafkka woyia lrkafka Wmmka;shlg tys m%Odk N+;d¾;fhka .=Kdx. Wreu jk ixl,amhhs'
    WodyrKhla f,i" .=rejrfhl=g ^Wm mka;shg& mqoa.,hdf.ka ^m%Odk N+;d¾;fhka& ku iy jhi jeks
    fmdÿ .=Kdx. Wreu jk kuq;a úIh jeks wu;r .=Kdx. o ;sìh yel' EER Wreuh ;=<" w;sßla;h
    wvq lrñka wod< N+;d¾; yryd fmdÿ .=Kdx. fnod .ekSug bv i,ihs'

    Specialization of entities / N+;d¾:j, úfYaIdhkh
    Specialization refers to the process of defining a more specific entity from a general one. For instance,
    Vehicle can be specialized into Car and Truck. Specialization defines entities that are more specific
    versions of a generalized entity. In EER specialization is used to model how entities can be divided into
    distinct, specialized groups based on shared attributes or behaviors.
    úfYaISlrKh hkq idudkH tllska jvd;a ksYaÑ; jia;=jla ks¾jpkh lsÍfï l%shdj,shhs' WodyrKhla
    f,i" jdykh" ld¾ iy g%la i|yd úfYaIs; úh yel' úfYaISlrKh u.ska idudkHlrKh jQ N+;d¾;hl
    jvd;a ksYaÑ; wkqjdo jk N+;d¾; ks¾jpkh lrhs' EER úfYaISlrKh ;=< yjq,a .=Kdx. fyda yeisÍï
    u; mokïj N+;d¾; fjkia" úfYaIs; lKavdhïj,g fnÈh yels wdldrh wdo¾Ykh lsÍug Ndú;d
    lrhs'

    Accordingly all these can be represented in an EER diagram. So, the correct answer number is 5.
    tA wkqj fuu ish,a,u EER igykl ksrEmKh l< yelsh' tuksid, ksjerÈ ms<s;=re wxlh 5 fõ'

    A Simple Explanation of Compiling, Linking, and Executing Code
    fla;h iïmdokh lsÍu" iïnkaO lsÍu iy l%shd;aul lsÍu ms<sn| ir, meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    1   Source Code / uQ,dY% fla;h
        This is the human-readable code you write, often in languages like C, C++ or Python. It contains
        instructions for the computer to follow. fuh fndfyda úg C, C++ fyda Python jeks NdIdj,ska
        ñksiqkag lshúh yels fla;hhs' mß.Klh wkq.ukh l< hq;= Wmfoia tys wvx.= fõ'
    2   Compiler / iïmdolh
        The compiler takes your source code and translates it into a language the computer can
        understand, which is usually machine code or assembly language. This translated code is called
        object code. iïmdolhd Tfí uQ,dY% fla;h f.k th mß.Klhg f;areï .; yels NdIdjlg
        mßj¾;kh lrhs" th idudkHfhka hka;% fla;h fyda tl,ia lsÍfï NdIdj fõ' fuu mßj¾;kh
        lrk ,o fla;h jia;= fla;h f,i ye¢kafõ'
    3   Object Code / jia;= fla;h
        Object code is the machine-readable version of your program. It contains instructions that the
        computer can execute, but it's often not complete. jia;= fla;h hkq Tfí jevigyfka hka;%fhka
        lshúh yels wkqjdohhs' mß.Klhg l%shd;aul l< yels Wmfoia tys wvx.= fõ" kuq;a th fndfyda
        úg iïmQ¾K fkdfõ'
    4   Linker / iïnkaOlh
        The linker combines multiple object code files and any necessary libraries into a single executable
        file. Libraries are collections of pre-written code that can be used in different programs.
        iïnkaOlh nyq jia;= fla; f.dkq iy ´kEu wjYH mqia;ld, ;ks l%shd;aul l< yels f.dkqjlg
        tAldnoaO lrhs' mqia;ld, hkq úúO jevigyka j, Ndú;d l< yels fmr ,sÅ; fla; tl;=jls'
    5   Executable File
        This is the final product that you can run on your computer. It contains all the code and resources
        needed for your program to work. When you double-click on it, the operating system loads it
        into memory and starts executing the instructions. fuh Tfí mß.Klfha l%shd;aul l< yels
        wjidk ksIamdokhhs' Tnf.a jevigyk l%shd;aul ùug wjYH ish¨u fla; iy iïm;a tys wvx.=
        fõ' Tn th u; fojrla la,sla l< úg" fufyhqï moaO;sh th u;lhg mgjd Wmfoia laßhd;aul
        lsÍu wdrïN lrhs'

    1) 1                   2) 14             3) 14.625         4) 15               5) 19

    Answer : 2 )
    Here, n is assigned the value 117.
    fuys§ n g 117 w.h mjrkq ,efí'

    m = (n & 127) // (2 ** 3)

    •   Step 1:
        Bitwise AND Operation

        The expression n & 127 performs a bitwise AND operation between n and 127.
        n & 127 m%ldYkh n iy 127 w;r ìgq wkqidrs AND fufyhqula isÿ lrhs'

        •    The binary representation of 117 is 01110101.
             117 ys oaúuh ksrEmKh 01110101 fõ'
        •    The binary representation of 127 is 01111111.
             127 ys oaúuh ksrEmKh 01111111 fõ'

        Now, let's perform the bitwise AND operation:
        oeka" wms ìgq wkqidrs AND fufyhqu isÿ lruq(

          01110101
        & 01111111
          --------------
          01110101

        The result of n & 127 is 117.
        n & 127 ys m%;sM,h 117 fõ'

    •   Step 2
        Division by Power of 2 / 2 n,fhka fn§u
        Next, we calculate 2 ** 3, which is 8.
        B<Õg" wms 2 ** 3 .Kkh lruq" th 8 fõ'
        m = 117 // 8

    •   Step 3
        Integer Division / ksÅ, fn§u
        117 // 8 = 14

        This means 117 divided by 8 is 14.625, here we discard the remainder in integer division.
        fuhska woyia lrkafka 117, 8 ka fn§u 14.625 fõ" fuys§ wms ksÅ, fn§fï§ b;sßh bj;,uq'

    Hence the answer is 14.

       lrkq ,efí'

 46. Which of the following is the correct example for CSS group selector?
     ldKav f;dard.ekSulg (group selector) ksjerÈ WodyrKh my; ljrla o?
    1)   h1{text-align:left ; color:blue;}
    2)   h1,h2{text-align:left , color:blue;}
    3)   h1.h2{text-align:left; color:blue;}
    4)   h1:h2{text-align:left; color:blue;}
    5)   h1,h2{text-align:left; color:blue;}

    Answer : 5 )
    What is a CSS Group Selector?
    A CSS group selector allows you to apply the same styles to multiple elements at once. This is done by
    listing the selectors separated by commas. For example, if you want to style both <h1> and <h2> elements
    with the same properties, you can use a group selector.
    CSS group selector uÕska Tng tljr nyqúO uQ,dx. i|yd tlu style fh§ug bv i,ihs' fuh isÿ
    lrkq ,nkafka fldudfjka fjka lrk ,o f;aÍï ,ehsia;=.; lsÍfuks' WodyrKhla f,i" Tng tlu
    .=Kdx. iys; <h1> iy <h2> uQ,dx. folu fudaia;r lsÍug wjYH kï" Tng lKavdhï f;aÍula
    Ndú;d l< yel'

    Key Points of a CSS Group Selector / CSS Group Selector m%Odk lreKq

    Syntax
    The syntax for a group selector is to list the selectors separated by commas, followed by the style rules
    enclosed in curly braces'
    group selector ldrlhla i|yd jk syntax tl jkafka i.,ka jryka ;=< we;s ú,di ks;s j,g wkqj
    fldudfjka fjka lrk ,o f;aÍï ,ehsia;=.; lsÍuhs'

    Purpose / wruqK
    This helps reduce redundancy in your CSS code, making it cleaner and more efficient.
    fuh Tnf.a CSS fla;fha w;sßla;h wvq lsÍug WmldÍ fõ" th msßisÿ yd jvd ld¾hlaIu lrhs'

    1) h1{text-align:left; color:blue;}
       This is a style rule for a single element (h1). It is not a group selector because it only targets one
       element.
       fuh ;ks uQ,dx.hla ^h1& i|yd jk ú,di Í;shls' th tla uQ,dx. muKla b,lal lrk neúka th
       group selector fkdfõ'

    2) h1,h2{text-align:left , color:blue;}
       This option correctly uses a group selector. It targets both h1 and h2 elements and applies the same
       styles (text-align:left and color:blue). However, there is a syntax error with the comma in
       color:blue; (it should be a semicolon).
       fuu úl,amh ksjerÈj group selector Ndú;d lrhs' th h1 iy h2 uQ,dx. folu b,lal lrk
       w;r tlu ú,dihka (text-align:left and color:blue). fhfoa' flfia fj;;a" fldudj
       color:blue; ^th semicolon úh hq;=h&'

    3) h1.h2{text-align:left; color:blue;}
       This is not a group selector. Instead, it targets elements that have both h1 and h2 classes (which is
       not valid in this context). It uses a class selector syntax incorrectly.
       fuh group selector fkdfõ' tA fjkqjg" th h1 iy h2 mka;s folu we;s uQ,dx. b,lal lrhs
       ^fuu ikao¾Nh ;=< j,x.= fkdfõ&' th mka;s f;aÍï syntax tlla jerÈ f,i Ndú;d lrhs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                             -85-                                        #ictfromabc

    4) h1:h2{text-align:left; color:blue;}
       This is also not a valid group selector. The syntax h1:h2 does not make sense in CSS. The colon (:)
       indicates a pseudo-class, which is not applicable here.
       fuho j,x.= lKavdhï f;aÍula fkdfõ' iskagelaia h1:h2 CSS ys f;areula ke;' colon ((& u.ska
       pseudo-class fmkakqï lrhs" th fuys wod< fkdfõ'

    5) h1,h2{text-align:left; color:blue;}
       This option is identical to option (2) and correctly uses a group selector, targeting both h1 and h2
       elements and applying the same styles. This option is correct.
       fuu úl,amh úl,amh 2 g iudk jk w;r h1 iy h2 uQ,dx. folu b,lal lr .ksñka group
       selector ksjerÈj Ndú;d lrhs' fuu úl,amh ksjerÈhs'

    The correct answer is: 5) h1,h2{text-align:left; color:blue;}

    This option correctly demonstrates the use of a CSS group selector, applying the same styles to both h1
    and h2 elements.

 50. What would be the output when the following PHP code is executed?
     my; oelafjk PHP fla;h l%shd;aul l< úg m%;sodkh l=ula fõ o?

    Arrays in PHP / PHP ys wrdjka
    An array is a data structure that stores one or more similar types of values in a single variable. PHP arrays
    are flexible and can hold values of different data types (integers, strings, objects, etc.).
    wrdjla hkq tla úp,Hhl iudk w.hka tlla fyda lsysmhla .nvd lrk o;a; jHqyhls' PHP wrd
    kuHYS,S jk w;r úúO o;a; j¾.j, ^mQ¾K ixLHd" strings" objects wdÈh& w.hka ;nd .; yel'

    Types of Arrays in PHP / PHP ys wrdjka j¾.

    1   Indexed Arrays iqÑ.; wrdjka
        Arrays with a numeric index. ixLHd;aul o¾Ylhla iys; wrd'
        E.g. $arr=array("Apple","Banana", "Cherry");
    2   Associative Arrays
        Arrays with named keys. kï lrk ,o h;=re iys; wrd'
        E.g. $arr=array("a" =>"Apple","b" => "Banana","c" =>"Cherry");
    3   Multidimensional Arrays
        Arrays containing one or more arrays. wrd tlla fyda lsysmhla wvx.= wrd'
        E.g. $arr = array(array("Apple", "Banana"), array("Cherry", "Date"));

    Accessing Array Elements: You access elements of an indexed array using numeric indices (starting from
    `0`), while for associative arrays, you use keys.This code demonstrates the use of an indexed array in PHP
    and how to access its elements by their indices.
    wrdjka uQ,dx. fj; m%fõY ùu( Tn ixLHd;aul o¾Yl Ndú;fhka iqÑ.; l< wrdjl uQ,dx. fj;
    m%fõY jk w;r ‘0’ isg wdrïN fõ" iydhl wrd i|yd" Tn h;=re Ndú;d lrhs' fuu fla;h PHP
    ys iqÑ.; l< wrdjla Ndú;d lsÍu iy tAjdfha uQ,dx. fj; m%fõY jk wdldrh ksrEmKh lrhs'

    Explanation of the Code

    PHP Array Definition / PHP Array w¾: oelaùu

    $class = array ("12-A", "12-B", "13-A");

    arrays are used to store multiple values in a single variable.
    fuu fma<sh uQ,dx. ;=kla wvx.= $class kï wrdjla ks¾udKh lrhs( "12-A", "12-B", iy "13-A".
    PHP ys" ;ks úp,Hhl nyq w.hka .nvd lsÍug wrd Ndú;d fõ'

    Accessing Array Elements and Concatenation.
    Array Elements iy Concatenation fj; m%fõY ùu
    echo "IT classes are ". $class[1] ." and ".$class[2];

    This line outputs a string by concatenating text with specific elements of the $class array. $class[1]
    accesses the second element of the array, which is "12-B". $class[2] accesses the third element of the
    array, which is "13-A".The echo statement combines these elements into a string and prints it.
    fuu fma<sh $class array ys ksYaÑ; uQ,dx. iuÕ mdG iïnkaO lsÍfuka string tlla m%;sodkh lrhs'
    $class[1] úiska "12-B" jk wrdfjys fojk uQ,dx. fj; m%fõY fõ' $class[2] wrdfjys ;=kajk

    uqøKh lrhs'

    meyeÈ,s lsÍu wkqj" PHP fla;fha m%;sodkh jkqfha(
    "IT classes are 12-B and 13-A"

      Software as a Service / uDÿldx. fiajdjla f,i (SaaS)

      Platform as a Service / miq;,hefiajdjla f,i (PaaS)
      Platform as a Service (PaaS) provides a cloud-based environment for developers to build, test, and deploy
      applications without worrying about managing the underlying infrastructure. It offers tools, middleware,
      and services needed for application development, making it easier to focus on coding rather than handling
      servers or storage. PaaS simplifies the development process by offering pre-configured environments that
      support various programming languages and frameworks, along with built-in scalability, load balancing,
      and security features. Examples include Google App Engine and Microsoft Azure.
      miq;,hefiajdjla f,i (PaaS) ixj¾Olhskag mj;sk há;, myiqlï l<ukdlrKh lsÍu .ek lror
      fkdù fhÿï ;ekSug" mÍlaId lsÍug iy fhoùug j,dl=¿ u; mokï jQ mßirhla imhhs' th fhÿï

      Microsoft Azure we;=<;a fõ'

      Infrastructure as a Service / há;, myiqlï fiajdjla f,i (IaaS)
      Infrastructure as a Service (IaaS) is a cloud service model that provides virtualized computing resources
      over the internet. It allows users to rent essential IT infrastructure—such as virtual machines, storage, and
      networking components—on a pay-as-you-go basis, giving them the flexibility to scale resources up or
      down as needed. IaaS offers the most control over the cloud environment, as users can manage and
      configure the virtualized hardware according to their specific needs. This service model is ideal for
      businesses that require high levels of customization and control. Examples include Amazon Web Services
      (AWS) and Microsoft Azure.
      há;, myiqlï fiajdjla f,i (IaaS) hkq wka;¾cd,h yryd w:;H l< mß.Kl iïm;a imhk

     Software as a Service / uDÿldx. fiajdjla f,i (SaaS)
     Software as a Service (SaaS) is a cloud computing model where software applications are delivered over
     the internet and accessed through a web browser. Users do not need to install or maintain the software on
     their local devices, as the service provider hosts, manages, and updates the software on their behalf. SaaS
     is commonly used for applications such as email, customer relationship management (CRM), and
     collaboration tools, offering a convenient, subscription-based model with automatic updates and
     maintenance handled by the provider. Examples include Google Workspace, Salesforce, and Dropbox.
     uDÿldx. fiajdjla f,i (SaaS) hkq j,dl=¿ fiajd wdlD;shla jk w;r tys§ uDÿldx. fhÿï

     Encryption / ixfla;kh
     Encryption is a process used to protect data by converting it into a code to prevent unauthorized access.
     This transformation involves using an algorithm and a key to encode the information, making it unreadable
     to anyone who does not have the key to decrypt it. Encryption is widely used to secure sensitive data, such
     as financial information, personal data, and communications, both during transmission and while stored.
     It plays a crucial role in ensuring confidentiality and data integrity in various digital environments.
     ixfla;kh hkq wkjirfhka m%fõY ùu je<elaùu i|yd fla;hla njg mßj¾;kh lsÍfuka o;a;
     wdrlaId lsÍug Ndú;d lrk l%shdj,shls' fuu mßj¾;khg f;dr;=re ixfla;kh lsÍug

     iy ikaksfõokhka jeks ixfõ§ o;a; iqrlaIs; lsÍug ixfla;kh nyq,j Ndú;d fõ' úúO äðg,a

     lrhs'

     Copyright / m%ldYk ysñlu
     Copyright is a legal protection granted to the creators of original works, such as literature, music, art, and
     software. It gives the creator exclusive rights to reproduce, distribute, perform, display, or license their
     work, typically for a certain period. Copyright aims to encourage creativity by ensuring that creators can
     control and benefit from their intellectual property. Infringing on copyright, such as copying or using
     someone else's work without permission, is illegal and can result in legal penalties.
     m%ldYk ysñlu hkq idys;Hh" ix.S;h" Ñ;%" iy uDÿldx. jeks uq,a lD;sj, ks¾udmlhkag ,nd § we;s
     kS;suh wdrlaIdjls' th idudkHfhka ksYaÑ; ld, iSudjla i|yd Tjqkaf.a ld¾hh m%;sksIamdokh lsÍug"

    Phishing / ;;=nEï
    Phishing is a type of cyber attack in which attackers attempt to deceive individuals into providing sensitive
    information, such as passwords, credit card numbers, or personal details. This is typically done through
    fraudulent emails, websites, or messages that appear to be from legitimate sources, such as banks, social
    networks, or trusted companies. Phishing attacks exploit human psychology, often using urgency or fear
    to trick victims into divulging confidential information, which can then be used for identity theft or
    financial fraud.
    ;;=nEï hkq uqrmo" fl%äÜ ldâ wxl fyda mqoa.,sl f;dr;=re jeks ixfõ§ f;dr;=re iemhSug m%ydrlhka
    mqoa.,hka /jàug W;aidy lrk ihsn¾ m%ydrhls' fuh idudkHfhka isÿ lrkq ,nkafka nexl=" iudc
    cd," fyda úYajdiodhS iud.ï jeks kS;Hdkql+, uQ,dY%j,ska fmfkk jxpksl Bfï,a" fjí wvú fyda
    m‚úv yrydh' ;;=nEï m%ydr udkj ufkdaúoHdj iQrdlEu" fndfyda úg yÈiaish fyda ìh Wmfhda.s lr
    .ksñka úkaÈ;hka ryis.; f;dr;=re fy<s lsÍug fmd<Ujhs" miqj th wkkH;d fidrlï lsÍu fyda
    uQ,H jxpdj i|yd Ndú;d l< yelsh'

    Plagiarism / fld,a,lEu
    Plagiarism is the act of using someone else's work, ideas, or expressions without proper acknowledgment,
    presenting them as one's own. This can include copying text, images, or code from another source without
    giving credit to the original author. Plagiarism is considered unethical and is often a violation of academic
    or professional integrity. In many educational and professional settings, it can lead to serious
    consequences, such as loss of reputation, legal penalties, or academic disciplinary actions.
    fld,a,lEu hkq fjk;a flfkl=f.a jev" woyia fyda m%ldYk ksis ms<s.ekSulska f;drj Ndú;d lrñka"
    tAjd ;udf.a hehs bÈßm;a lsÍuhs' uq,a l;=jrhdg oekqï §ulska f;drj fjk;a uQ,dY%hlska fm<" mska;+r
    fyda fla;h msgm;a lsÍu fuhg we;=<;a úh yel' fld,a,lEu iodpdr úfrdaë f,i i,lkq ,nk w;r
    th fndfyda úg Ydia;%Sh fyda jD;a;Sh wLKav;dj W,a,x>kh lsÍuls' fndfyda wOHdmksl iy jD;a;Sh

    Software Piracy / uDÿldx. fld,a,lEu
    Software piracy is the unauthorized copying, distribution, or use of software. This illegal activity involves
    duplicating software without purchasing the proper licenses, sharing software with others, or using cracked
    versions of programs to bypass licensing restrictions. Software piracy undermines the revenue of software
    developers and companies, leading to economic losses and potentially harming the development of new
    software. It is considered a serious offense and is punishable by law, with penalties ranging from fines to
    imprisonment.
    uDÿldx. fld,a,lEu hkq uDÿldx. wkjirfhka msgm;a lsÍu" fnod yeÍu fyda Ndú;hhs' fuu kS;s

    whether the development team has the necessary technical expertise. It evaluates whether the required
    technology, tools, and resources are available and whether the team can successfully apply them.

    aspects of implementation, such as user acceptance, ease of use, and the alignment of the system with the
    organization's operational goals.

    Payment Gateway / f.ùï fodrgqj

    A Payment Gateway is a technology that facilitates online transactions by acting as an intermediary
    between a merchant's website and the financial institutions involved. It securely processes, authorizes, and
    transfers payment information, enabling customers to complete purchases on e-commerce platforms. The
    gateway encrypts sensitive data, such as credit card details, to ensure the security of the transaction, and
    communicates with the acquiring bank to approve or decline the payment based on various factors.

 (b) Complete the four (4) blanks (indicated by ……) in the following Python program to calculate the
     factorial of an integer.
     ksì,hl l%udfrdams;h (factorial) .Kkh lsÍug wod< my; mhs;ka fla;fha ysia;eka y;r (……
     j,ska oelafjk) iïmQ¾K lrkak'

     Note: The factorial of a positive integer is defined as the product of that integer and all the integers
           below it. e.g., factorial of 4 is equal to 1 x 2 x 3 x 4 = 24. The factorial of 0 is defined as 1.
     igyk: Ok ksÅ,hl l%udfrdams;h hkq tu ksì,fha yd Bg wvq ish¨ ksì,j, .=Ks;hhs' Wod( 4 ys
           l%udfrdams;h 1x 2 x 3 x 4 = 24 g iudk fõ' Bg wu;rj 0 ys l%udfrdams;h 1 f,i w¾: oelafõ'

       # Get input from user
       num = int(input("Enter a number:"))
       factorial = 1
       if num < 0:
              print("Factorial is not defined for negative numbers!")
       elif num == 0 :
              print("The factorial of 0 is 1")
       else:
              for i in range(1,num + 1):
                      factorial *= i
       print ("The factorial of ",num,"is", factorial)

 (c) Consider the following Python program:
     my; mhs;ka l%uf,aLh i,lkak'

      lower = 2
      upper = 5

      for num in range(lower, upper + 1):
            flag = 1
            if num > 1:
                  for i in range(2, num):
                        if (num % i) = = 0:
                              flag = 0
                              break
                  if flag = = 1:
                        print(num)

    Write the output of the above program.
    by; l%uf,aLfha m%;sodkh ,shd olajkak'

    Answer :
    2
    3
    5

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    1. Range Setup / mrdi ieliqu
       The loop iterates over the numbers from lower (2) to upper + 1 (6), so the values of num will
       be 2, 3, 4 and 5.
       Æmh my<(2) isg by< + 1 (6) olajd ixLHd u; mqkrdj¾;kh fõ" tneúka num ys w.hka 2,
       3, 4 iy 5 jkq we;'

    2. Iteration Details / mqkrdj¾;k úia;r

        For num = 2
        flag is set to 1.
        flag tl 1 f,i ilid we;'
        Since num is greater than 1, the inner loop checks for factors from 2 to 1 (which means it doesn't
        run).
        num 1 g jvd jeä neúka" wNHka;r Æmh 2 isg 1 olajd idOl i|yd mÍlaId lrhs ^thska woyia
        jkafka th l%shd;aul fkdjk njhs&'
        flag remains 1, so 2 is printed.
        flag, 1 f,i mj;S" tneúka 2 uqøKh flf¾'

        For num = 3:
        flag is set to 1.
        flag tl 1 f,i ilid we;'
        The inner loop checks for factors from 2 to 2 (it only checks i = 2).
        wNHka;r Æmh 2 isg 2 olajd idOl i|yd mÍlaId lrhs ^th i = 2 muKla mÍlaId lrhs&'
        3 % 2 is not equal to 0, so the loop does not change flag.
        3 % 2, 0 g iudk fkdfõ" tneúka Æmh flag tl fjkia fkdlrhs'
        flag remains 1, so 3 is printed.
        flag, 1 f,i mj;S" tneúka 3 uqøKh flf¾'

       For num = 4
       flag is set to 1.
       flag tl 1 f,i ilid we;'
       The inner loop checks for factors from 2 to 3.
       wNHka;r Æmh 2 isg 3 olajd idOl i|yd mÍlaId lrhs'
       For i = 2, 4 % 2 equals 0, indicating that 4 is divisible by 2
       i = 2 i|yd" 4 % 2, 0 g iudk fõ" 4, 2 ka fnÈh yels nj fmkakqï lrhs'
       flag is set to 0, and the loop breaks.
       flag 0 f,i ilid we;s w;r" Æmh wjidk fú'
       Since flag is now 0, 4 is not printed.
       oeka flag tl 0 neúka" 4 uqøKh fkdflf¾'

       flag is set to 1.
       flag tl 1 f,i ilid we;'
       The inner loop checks for factors from 2 to 4.
       wNHka;r Æmh 2 isg 4 olajd idOl i|yd mÍlaId lrhs'
       For i = 2, 5 % 2 is not equal to 0. / i = 2 i|yd 5 % 2 g iudk fkdfõ'
       For i = 3, 5 % 3 is not equal to 0. / i = 3 i|yd 5 % 3 g iudk fkdfõ'
       For i = 4, 5 % 4 is not equal to 0. / i = 4 i|yd 5 % 4 g iudk fkdfõ'
       Since no divisors were found, flag remains 1, so 5 is printed.

    When a doctor at a counter in the clinic is ready, s/he selects the next patient according to the PRESENT

 (a) If a maximum of 20 patients are to be examined by the clinic doctors per an hour, write down one
     (1) functional requirement with respect to appointment scheduling.
     idhk ffjoHjre Wmßu jYfhka tla mehlg frda.Ska 20 la mÍlaId l< hq;= kï" frda.Skaf.a bÈß
     meñKSï Èkhka yd fõ,djka kshulrKhg wod< tla (1) ld¾h noaO wjYH;djhla (functional
     requirement) ,shd olajkak'

    Functional requirements / ld¾hnoaO wjYH;d

    Functional requirements specify what the system should do. They define the specific behaviors, features,
    and functions of the system. These requirements describe how the system will interact with users, other
    systems, and its environment, detailing the operations it must perform.
    ld¾hnoaO wjYH;d" moaO;sh l=ula l< hq;=o hkak i|yka lrhs' fïjd moaO;sfha ksYaÑ; yeisÍï"

    mßirh iuÕ wka;¾l%shd lrk wdldrh úia;r lrhs" th l< hq;= fufyhqï úia;r lrhs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -14-                                          #ictfromabc

    For this a functional requirement can be written with the following meaning.
    fï i|yd my; wdldrfha f;areu iys; ld¾hnoaO wjYH;djla ,súh yelsh'

    The system must ensure that no more than 20 patients are scheduled for appointments with the clinic
    doctors within any given hour.
    ´kEu mehla we;=<; idhk ffjoHjreka iuÕ yuqùï i|yd frda.Ska 20lg jvd jeä m%udKhla ie,iqï
    lr fkdue;s nj moaO;sh iy;sl l< hq;=uh'

    This requirement ensures that the system limits the number of appointments to 20 per hour, preventing an
    overload on the clinic doctors. It helps maintain efficient patient flow and ensures that the doctors have
    enough time to examine each patient properly. If 20 appointments are already booked in a specific hour,
    the system should block any further bookings for that time slot and suggest alternative available slots.
    fuu wjYH;djh uÕska mehlg frda.Ska ixLHdj 20 olajd iSud lrk nj iy;sl lrhs" tu.ska idhk
    ffjoHjreka u; wêl mSvkhla megùu j<lajhs' th ld¾hlaIu frda.S m%jdyhla mj;ajd .ekSug

 (b) The hospital expects to avoid a long queue of people being formed at the clinic reception for the
     validity check. Write down one (1) non-functional requirement with respect to that need.
     j,x.=;d mÍlaIKh i|yd idhk ms<s.ekSï ljqkagrh wi, frda.Skaf.a È.= fmda,sula we;s fkdùu
     frdayf,a n,dfmdfrd;a;=jhs' tu wfmalaIdjg wod< tla (1) ld¾h noaO fkdjk wjYH;djhla (non-
     functional requirement) ,shd olajkak'

    Non-functional requirements / ld¾hnoaO fkdjk wjYH;d

    Non-functional requirements describe how the system performs its functions rather than what it does. They
    encompass the quality attributes, performance standards, and constraints that the system must meet. This
    includes aspects like security, performance, usability, reliability, and scalability.

6.(a) Parity Check is a simple technique to detect errors in data communications.
      iu;d mÍlaIdj hkq o;a; ikaksfõokfh§ isÿjk fodaI fidhd.ekSfu ir, Ys,amSh l%uhls'
     Assume the seven bits 1010110 need to be transmitted. Explain how the odd parity check can be
     performed to detect any error in its transmission.
     1010110 hk ìgq y; iïfma%IKh l< hq;= hehs is;kak" th iïfma%IKfha§ hï fodaIhla isÿj we;soehs
     fidhdne,Sug T;af;a iu;d (odd parity) mÍlaIdj isÿ l< yels whqre myokak'

     Parity Check / iu;d mÍlaIdj
     Parity checking is a basic error detection technique used in digital communication systems to ensure
     that data is transmitted without errors. It works by adding an extra bit, called the parity bit, to a set
     of data bits. The role of the parity bit is to make sure that the total number of 1's in the data (including
     the parity bit) follows a certain rule, which can be either odd or even parity.
     iu;d mÍlaIdj hkq o;a; fodaIhlska f;drj iïfm%aIKh jk nj iy;sl lsÍu i|yd wxls; ikaksfõok
     moaO;sj, Ndú;d lrk uQ,sl fodaI y÷kd.ekSfï l%uhls' th l%shd lrkafka o;a; ìgq ldKavhlg iu;d
     ìgq f,i ye¢kafjk wu;r ìgqjla tl;= lsÍfuks' iudkd;au;d ìgqfjys ld¾hNdrh jkafka o;a;j,
     we;s uq¿ 1 ixLHdj ^iu;d ìgqjo we;=¿j& T;af;a fyda brÜfÜ iudk úh yels ksYaÑ; Í;shla wkq.ukh
     lrk njg j. n,d .ekSuhs'

     There are two main types of parity checking / iudkd;au;djh msßlaiSfï m%Odk j¾. folla ;sfí'

     1. Even Parity / brÜfÜ iu;d mrslaIdj
        In even parity, the parity bit is added in such a way that the total number of 1's in the data
        (including the parity bit) is even.
        brÜfÜ iu;d mrslaIdfjos" o;a;j, we;s uq¿ 1 ixLHdj ^iu;d ìgqjo we;=¿j& brÜfÜ jk
        wdldrhg iu;d ìgqjla tl;= fõ'
         If the number of 1's in the original data is already even, the parity bit is set to 0. If the number of
         1's is odd, the parity bit is set to 1.
         uq,a o;a;j, 1 ixLHdj oekgu;a brÜfÜ kï" iu;d ìgqj 0 f,i ilihs' 1 ixLHdj T;af;a kï"
         iu;d ìgqj 1 f,i ilihs'

     Example / WodyrKh
     • Data (o;a;) : 110011 (4 ones)
        Since there are already an even number of 1's, the parity bit is 0.
        oekgu;a 1 ys brÜfÜ ixLHdjla we;s neúka" iu;d ìgqj 0 fõ'
     •   Transmitted data with parity bit (iu;d ìgqj iuÕ iïfm%aIKh l< o;a;) : 1100110
         If the number of 1's in the data changes during transmission, it will no longer be even, and the
         error will be detected.
         iïfm%aIKh w;r;=r o;a;j, 1 ys ixLHdj fjkia jqjfyd;a" th ;jÿrg;a iudk fkdjk w;r
         fodaIh wkdjrKh fõ'

     2. Odd Parity / T;af;a iu;d mrslaIdj
        In odd parity, the parity bit is added to make the total number of 1's odd.
        T;af;a iu;d mrslaIdfjos" 1 ys uq¿ ixLHdj T;af;a lsÍug iu;d ìgq tl;= lrkq ,efí'
         If the number of 1's in the original data is even, the parity bit is set to 1. If the number of 1's is
         odd, the parity bit is set to 0.
         uq,a o;a;j, 1 ys ixLHdj brÜfÜ kï" iu;d ìgqj 1 f,i ilihs' 1 ys ixLHdj T;af;a kï"
         iu;d ìgqj 0 f,i ilihs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                 -4-                                            #ictfromabc

    Example / WodyrK
    • Data (o;a;) : 101010 (3 ones)
       The number of 1's is odd, so the parity bit is 0.
       1 ys ixLHdj T;af;a" tneúka iu;d ìgqj 0 fõ'
    •   Transmitted data with parity bit (iu;d ìgqj iuÕ iïfma%IKh l< o;a;) : 1010100
        If the number of 1's becomes even due to a transmission error, the system can detect it.
        iïfm%aIK fodaIhla fya;=fjka 1 ys ixLHdj brÜfÜ kï" moaO;shg th y÷kd.; yelsh

    Below shows, how the odd parity check can be performed.
    T;af;a iu;d mÍlaIdj isÿ l< yels wdldrh my; mrsos fõ'

    1. Count the number of 1's in the data bits / o;a; ìgq j, 1 ys ixLHdj .Kka lruq
       The binary sequence 1010110 consists of four '1' bits, which are located at positions 1, 3, 5 and 6.
       1010110 jk oaúuh wkql%uh 1 ys ìgq y;rlska iukaú; jk w;r tAjd 1, 3, 5 iy 6 hk ia:dkj,
       msysgd we;'

    2. Determine the parity bit / iudkd;au;djh ;SrKh lruq
       Since odd parity is required, the total number of 1's, including the parity bit, should be odd. Currently,
       there are four 1's, which is an even number. To make the number of 1's odd, we add a parity bit with
       the value 1.
       T;af;a iu;d mrslaIdj wjYH neúka" iu;d ìgqj we;=¿j 1 ys uq¿ ixLHdj T;af;a úh hq;=h'
       oekg" 1 y;rla we;" th brÜfÜ wxlhls' 1 ys ixLHdj T;af;a lsÍug" wms 1 w.h iuÕ iu;d
       ìgqj tlla tl;= lruq'

    3. Transmit the data with the parity bit / iu;d ìgqj iuÕ o;a; iïmafraIKh lrkak
       The data to be transmitted, including the parity bit, is 10101101 (the original 7 bits plus the parity bit).
       iu;d ìgqj we;=¿j iïfm%aIKh l< hq;= o;a; ^10101101 uq,a ìgq 7 iy iu;d ìgqj& fõ'

    4. Error detection at the receiver / .%dylfha fodaI y÷kd.ekSu
       After receiving the data, the receiver will count the number of 1's in the received message. If the
       number of 1's is odd, it assumes no error occurred. If the number of 1's is even, it indicates that an
       error has occurred during transmission.
       o;a; ,eîfuka miq" .%dylhd ,enqKq mKsúvfha 1 .Kk .Kka lrhs' 1 ys ixLHdj T;af;a kï" th
       lsisÿ fodaIhla isÿ fkdjQ nj Wml,amkh lrhs' 1 ys ixLHdj brÜfÜ kï" th iïfm%aIKh lsÍfï§
       fodaIhla isÿù we;s nj fmkakqï lrhs'

    In summary, the odd parity check adds a parity bit to ensure the total number of 1's is odd. This way, if the
    data changes during transmission (for example, if a bit is flipped), the error can be detected.

     filtering communication traffic and ensuring that only authorized data can enter or leave network.

 (b) The following description is about myShopper, a multi-agent system which enables a buyer to search
     the entire online marketplace for the best products. In addition to the price, reviews by other buyers,
     special offers, reputations of merchants and the lengths and types of warranties are also considered.
     iuia; ud¾..; fjf<|fmd<u msßlaid fyd|u NdKav fiùug .ekqïlrejkag ux i,ik myShopper
     nyq-tacka; moaO;sh .ek úiakrhla my; oelafõ' ñ,g wu;rj" wfkl=;a .ekqïlrejkaf.a úpdr o"
     úfYaI §ukd o" fjf<kaokaf.a lS¾;s kdu o" j.lSu ld, iSud iy j¾.hka o ie,ls,a,g .efka'

     When a user (buyer) accesses the myShopper website, a chat-bot agent starts interacting with the
     user. User can use voice or text as the input medium to give his/her requirements for a product.
     During the interaction, the chat-bot passes the extracted information to a search-agent who will
     takeover the search for the best product for the user. For this, the search-agent will start several
     domain-agents specifying each of them the requirements of the user and specific domains (web sites)
     to search in. To speedup the search, each domain-agent will start several sub-agents to search sub-
     domains under its main domain. After the search, each sub-agent will pass the appropriate results
     back to its parent domain-agent. Once all such results from the sub-agents are received, each domain-
     agent compares them and submits the best results to the search-agent. The search-agent will then
     compare all such results and gives the details of the best product back to the chat-bot. The chat-bot
     will then display it to the user as text.
     mßYS,lhd ^.ekqïlre& myShopper fjí wvúhg msúis úg" ixjdofha fhfok chat-bot frdfnda
     tackakjrfhla mßYS,l iu. wka;¾ l%shdj wrUhs' mßYS,lg lgyË (voice) fyda mdG (text) fyda
     wdodk udOH f,i Ndú; lr NdKavh ms<sn| ;u wjYH;djhka ,ndÈh yel' fuu wka;¾l%shdj w;r;=r
     chat-bot tacka; úiska Wlyd .kq ,nk f;dr;=re fijqï tacka;jrhl=g Ndr lrkq ,nk w;r" mßYS,l

    Multi-Agent System / nyq-tAcka; moaO;sh (MAS)
    A multi-agent system (MAS) is a system composed of multiple interacting agents, which are autonomous
    entities that can perceive their environment, make decisions, and take actions to achieve individual or
    collective goals. These agents can collaborate, compete, or work independently, depending on the system's
    objectives. MAS is often used in complex problem-solving scenarios, such as robotics, simulations, and
    distributed computing, where tasks are too large or intricate for a single agent to handle. The agents in
    MAS can communicate and cooperate to achieve better overall system performance, leading to more
    efficient and adaptive solutions.
    nyq-tAcka; moaO;sh (MAS) hkq nyq wka;¾l%shdldÍ ksfhdað;hkaf.ka iukaú; moaO;shls" tAjd ;u
    mßirh jgyd .ekSug" ;SrK .ekSug iy mqoa., fyda iduQysl wruqKq idlaId;a lr .ekSu i|yd l%shd
    l< yels iajdëk wdh;k fõ' fuu ksfhdað;hskag moaO;sfha wruqKq wkqj iyfhda.fhka lghq;= lsÍug"
    ;rÕ lsÍug fyda iajdëkj lghq;= lsÍug yelsh' MAS fndfyda úg frdfnda úoHdj" iudlrK iy fnod
    yßk ,o mß.Kkh jeks ixlS¾K .eg¿ úi£fï wjia:d j,§ Ndú;d fõ" tys§ ld¾hhka ;ks
    ksfhdað;fhl=g yeisrùug fkdyels ;rï úYd, fyda ixlS¾K fõ' MAS ys ksfhdað;hskag jvd fyd|

    Why Develop Sub-Agents /Wmtcka;jrhka ixj¾Okh lrkafka wehs ?
    Sub-agents are developed to handle specific tasks or subtasks within a larger system, allowing for better
    distribution of responsibilities and more efficient problem-solving. By delegating smaller, focused tasks
    to sub-agents, the overall system becomes more scalable and adaptable to complex environments. Sub-
    agents can operate independently or in coordination with other agents, improving the system’s ability to
    manage intricate processes and respond to dynamic situations. This modular approach ensures that each
    agent can specialize in its assigned function, resulting in more efficient and streamlined operations.
    j.lSï jvd fyd¢ka fnod yeÍug iy jvd;a ld¾hlaIu .eg¿ úi£ug bv i,iñka úYd, moaO;shla
    ;=< ksYaÑ; ld¾hhka fyda Wm ld¾hhka yeisrùug WmtAcka;jreka ixj¾Okh lr we;' l=vd" wjOdkh
    fhduq l< ld¾hhka WmtAcka;jreka fj; mejÍfuka" iuia; moaO;sh jvd;a mßudKh l< yels w;r
    ixlS¾K mßirhkag wkqj¾;kh fõ' WmtAcka;jrekag iajdëkj fyda fjk;a tAcka;jrhka iuÕ
    iïnkaëlrKfhka l%shd l< yels w;r" ixlS¾K l%shdj,Ska l<ukdlrKh lsÍug iy .;sl ;;ajhkag
    m%;spdr oelaùug moaO;sfha yelshdj jeä ÈhqKq lrhs' fuu fudähq,¾ m%fõYh uÕska tla tla
    tAcka;jrhkag tys mejÍ we;s ld¾hfha úfYaI;ajh ,nd .; yels nj iy;sl lrk w;r" tys
    m%;sM,hla f,i jvd;a ld¾hlaIu iy úêu;a fufyhqï isÿ fõ'

     Understanding the Problem / .eg¨j f;areï .ekSu
     1. Input / wdodkh
         •    A list L containing the ages of students.
              isiqkaf.a jhi wvx.= L ,ehsia;=j.
         •    An integer k representing the age threshold.
              jhia iSudj ksfhdackh lrk k ksÅ,.

     2. Output / m%;sodkh
         •    The average age of the students in list L who are younger than k.
              k g jvd nd, L ,ehsia;=fõ isák isiqkaf.a idudkH jhi'

     Steps to Solve the Problem / .eg¨j úi£ug mshjr
     1. Filter the Ages / jhia f;dard .eksu
         •    First, we need to identify students whose ages are less than k.
              m<uqj" jhi wjqreÿ k g wvq isiqka y÷kd .; hq;=h'
         •    This involves iterating through the list L and selecting only those ages that satisfy this condition.
              fuhg L ,ehsia;=j yryd mqkrdj¾;kh lsÍu iy fuu fldkafoaish ;Dma;su;a lrk jhia
              muKla f;dard .ekSu we;=<;a fõ'

     2. Calculate the Average / idudkHh .Kkh lsrsu
         •    Sum the ages of the selected students.
              f;dard.;a isiqkaf.a jhi tl;= lrkak
         •    Count the number of students who meet the age condition.
              jhia fldkafoais imqrd,k isiqka ixLHdj .Kkh lrkak'
         •    Calculate the average by dividing the total sum by the count.
              uq¿ tl;=j .Kkska fn§fuka idudkHh .Kkh lrkak'

  (i) What would be the output if L1 = 2, 4, 7, 9, 3, 5 and L2 = 1, 3, 8, 9, 6, 5, 7?
      L1=2, 4, 7, 9, 3, 5 iy L2=1, 3, 8, 9, 6, 5, 7 fõ kï" m%;sodkh l=ula fõ o?

     Answer / ms<s;=r

     The output would be 4.
     m%;sodkh 4 jkq we;'

     The flowchart determines how many elements from L1 are also present in L2. To find this,
     .e,Sï igyk L1 ys uQ,dx. lShla L2 ys o ;sfío hkak ;SrKh lrhs' fuh fidhd .ekSug,

     1. Compare Elements / uQ,dx. ii|kak

             2 from L1   is not present in L2
                         L2 ys fkdue;'
             4 from L1   is not present in L2.
                         L2 ys fkdue;'
             9 from L1   is present in L2.
                         L2 ys mj;S'
             3 from L1   is present in L2.
                         L2 ys mj;S'
             5 from L1   is present in L2.
                         L2 ys mj;S'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                              -18-                                         #ictfromabc

    2. Count Matches / iudk b,lalï .Kka lrkak
        The elements that are common between L1 and L2 are 7, 9, 3 and 5.
        L1 iy L2 w;r fmdÿ uQ,dx. jkafka 7, 9, 3 iy 5 h'

    3. Result / m%;sM,h
        Since there are 4 common elements, the output of the algorithm is 4.
        fmdÿ uQ,dx. 4la we;s neúka we,af.dß;ufha m%;sodkh 4 fõ'

 (ii) What is the purpose of this algorithm?
      uu we,af.dß;ufhys wruqK l=ula o?

    The purpose of this algorithm is to count the number of elements that are common to both lists L1 and L2.
    fuu we,af.dß;ufha wruqK jkafka L1 iy L2 ,ehsia;= folgu fmdÿ uQ,dx. ixLHdj .Kkh lsÍuhs'

    •   The algorithm takes two lists, L1 and L2, as input.
        we,af.dß;u wdodkh f,i L1 iy L2 ,ehsia;= folla .kS'
    •   It iterates through each element of L1 and compares it with all elements of L2.
        th L1 ys tla tla uQ,dx. yryd mqkrdj¾;kh jk w;r L2 ys ish¨u uQ,dx. iuÕ ixikaokh
        lrhs'
    •   For every match found (i.e., an element from L1 that is also in L2), it increments a counter.
        fidhd.kakd ,o iEu .e,mSula i|ydu ^tkï" L1 yd L2 ys we;s uQ,dx.hla&" th ljqkagrhla jeä
        lrhs'
    •   The algorithm continues this process until all elements of L1 have been checked.
        L1 ys ish¨u uQ,dx. mÍlaId lrk f;la we,af.dß;u fuu l%shdj,sh È.gu lrf.k hhs'

 (iii) Develop a Python program to implement the algorithm expressed by the flowchart.
       .e,Sï igyfkys m%ldY jk we,af.dß;uh l%shd;aul lsÍu i|yd mhs;ka l%uf,aLhla f.dvk.kak'

    def count_common_elements(L1, L2):
        count = 0
        i1 = 0
        N1 = len(L1)

          while i1 < N1:
              i2 = 0
              N2 = len(L2)
              while i2 < N2:
                  if L1[i1] == L2[i2]:
                      count += 1
                      break
                  i2 += 1
              i1 += 1

          return count

    inL1 = input("Enter the elements in L1 : ")
    L1 = [int(x) for x in inL1.split()]
    inL2 = input("Enter the elements in L2 : ")
    L2 = [int(x) for x in inL2.split()]
    print(count_common_elements(L1, L2))

    inL1 = input("Enter the elements in L1 : ")
    L1 = [int(x) for x in inL1.split()]
    inL2 = input("Enter the elements in L2 : ")
    L2 = [int(x) for x in inL2.split()]

    for i1 in L1:
         for i2 in L2:
              if i1 == i2:
                  count = count + 1
    print(count)

 (ii) In which normal form does the Contracts table exist?
      by; Contracts j.=j l=uk m%u;lrKfhys mj;S o=?
    The table is in First Normal Form (1NF) because it has atomic values, and there are no repeating groups.
    The primary key of the table is CNo (contract number).
    j.=j mrudKql w.hka we;s ksid;a" mqkrdj¾;k lKavdhï fkdue;s ksid;a m<uq m%;ulrK wdldrfhka
    (1NF) we;' j.=fõ m%d:ñl h;=r CNo (contract number) fõ'
    There are no partial dependencies because all non-key attributes (like AName, AMobile, Client) depend
    on the entire primary key (CNo) and not on a part of the key. Although the agent’s details could be moved
    to a separate table to avoid redundancy, there is no violation of 2NF since no non-key attribute depends
    only on a part of the key.
    ish¨u m%Odk fkdjk .=Kdx. ^AName, AMobile, Client jeks& h;=fra fldgila u; fkdj iïmQ¾K
    m%d:ñl h;=r (CNo) u; r|d mj;sk ksid w¾O mrdh;a;;d fkdue;' w;sßla; ùu j<lajd .ekSu i|yd
    ksfhdað;hdf.a úia;r fjku j.=jlg f.k hd yels jqjo" 2NF W,a,x>khla fkdue;" ukao h;=r
    fkdjk .=Kdx.hla h;=fra fldgila u; muKla r|d mj;S'
    So, the table is indeed in 2NF but not in 3NF, as some attributes (like agent details) depend on another
    non-primary attribute (ACode).
    tneúka" j.=j we;a; jYfhkau 2NF ys we;s kuq;a 3NF ys fkdfõ" iuyr .=Kdx. ^ksfhdað; úia;r
    jeks& fjk;a m%d:ñl fkdjk .=Kdx.hla (ACode) u; r|d mj;S'
    So, the answer is 2NF.
    tneúka" ms<s;=r 2NF fõ'

 (iii) Convert the Contracts table into next normal form. (It is not necessary to write the data in derived
       relations in the next normal form.)
       Contracts j.=j B<Õ m%u;lrKhg yrjkak' ^B<Õ m%u;lrKfha§ jHq;amkak jk j.=j, o;a; ,sùu
       wkjYH fõ'&

     To move the Contracts table into the next normal form, Third Normal Form (3NF), we need to remove
     any transitive dependencies. A transitive dependency occurs when a non-key attribute depends on another
     non-key attribute rather than directly on the primary key.
     Contracts j.=j ó<Õ idudkH fmdaruh" ;=kajk idudkH fmdaruh (3NF) fj; f.k hdug" wmg lsishï

        Multicore processors allow for parallel processing, meaning multiple tasks can be executed
        simultaneously. This is particularly beneficial for applications designed to take advantage of multiple
        cores, resulting in faster processing times and improved performance for tasks such as video editing,
        3D rendering, and complex calculations.

        With multiple cores, a computer can handle several applications at once without significant
        performance degradation. Each application can run on a separate core, allowing for smoother
        multitasking experiences. This is especially useful in environments where users frequently switch
        between applications or run multiple programs simultaneously.

    •   Energy Efficiency / n,Yla;s ld¾hlaIu;dj
        Multicore processors can be more energy-efficient than single-core processors. They can perform the
        same amount of work with less power by distributing tasks across cores, allowing each core to operate
        at a lower frequency and voltage. This results in reduced power consumption and heat generation,

 (iii) Explain what is meant by volatile memory and write down one (1) example for such selecting from
            the
       list below.
       kYH (volatile) u;lh hkafkka l=ula woyia fõoehs myod tjekaklg WodyrK tlla (1) my;
       ,ehsia;=fjka f;dard ,shkak'
     List: { Dynamic RAM (DRAM), Hard disk, L1 cache, Registers }
     ,ehsia;=j: { .;sl iiïNdù m%fõY u;lh (DRAM), oDV äialh, L1 ksys; u;lh, frðia;r }

     Volatile and non-volatile memory are two fundamental types of computer memory that differ primarily in
     their data retention capabilities.
     kYH iy kYH fkdjk u;lh uQ,sl jYfhka tAjdfha o;a; r|jd .ekSfï yelshdfjka fjkia jk uQ,sl
     mß.Kl u;l j¾. folls'

     •   Volatile Memory / kYH u;lh
         Volatile memory is a type of computer memory that requires a continuous power supply to maintain
         the stored information. When the power is turned off, all data stored in volatile memory is lost. This
         memory is typically used for tasks that require fast access to data, such as running applications and
         processing active tasks.
         kYH u;lh hkq .nvd lr we;s f;dr;=re mj;ajd .ekSu i|yd wLKav n, iemhqula wjYH jk
         mß.Kl u;l j¾.hls' úÿ,sh úikaê l< úg"kYH u;lfha .nvd lr we;s ish¨u o;a; ke;s
         ù hhs' fuu u;lh idudkHfhka Ndú; jkafka fhÿï Odjkh lsÍu iy il%sh ld¾hhka ieliSu
         jeks o;a; fj; fõ.j;a m%fõYhla wjYH jk ld¾hhka i|yd h'

         Examples / WodyrK

         •   Dynamic RAM (DRAM) : A common type of volatile memory used in computers and devices
             for temporary data storage.
             .;sl iiïndú m%fõY u;lh (DRAM) ( ;djld,sl o;a; .nvd lsÍu i|yd mß.Kl iy
             Wmdx.j, Ndú;d jk fmdÿ kYH u;l j¾.hls'

         •   L1 Cache : A small amount of very fast memory located inside the CPU, used to store frequently
             accessed data and instructions for quick retrieval.
             L1 ksys; u;lh ( CPU ;=< msysgd we;s b;d fõ.j;a u;lfha l=vd m%udKhla" ks;r m%fõY
             jk o;a; iy blauka ,nd .ekSu i|yd Wmfoia .nvd lsÍug Ndú;d lrhs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                -30-                                           #ictfromabc

         •   Registers : Small storage locations within the CPU that hold data temporarily during processing.
             frðia;r( ieliqï w;r;=r o;a; ;djld,slj r|jd ;nd .kakd CPU ;=< l=vd .nvd ia:dk'

     •   Non-Volatile Memory / kYH fkdjk u;lh
         Non-volatile memory, in contrast, retains stored data even when the power is turned off. This type of
         memory is essential for long-term data storage, as it allows information to persist across power cycles.
         Non-volatile memory is generally slower than volatile memory but is crucial for storing operating
         systems, applications, and user data.
         kYH fkdjk u;lh" Bg fjkiaj" n,h wl%sh jQ úg mjd .nvd l< o;a; r|jd ;nd .kS' È.=
         ld,Sk o;a; .nvd lsÍu i|yd fuu j¾.fha u;lh w;HjYH fõ" ukao th f;dr;=re n, pl%
         yryd mej;Sug bv i,ihs' kYH fkdjk u;lh idudkHfhka kYH u;lhg jvd ukao.dó jk
         kuq;a fufyhqï moaO;s" fhÿï iy mßYS,l o;a; .nvd lsÍu i|yd b;d jeo.;a fõ'

         Examples / WodyrK

         • Hard Disk : A non-volatile storage device used for long-term data storage, retaining information
           even when the computer is powered down.
           oDV ;eáh ( È.=ld,Sk o;a; .nvd lsÍu i|yd Ndú;d lrk kYH fkdjk .nvd Wmdx.hla"
           mß.Klh n, rys; jQ úg mjd f;dr;=re r|jd ;nd .kS'

     In summary, volatile memory is fast and temporary, suitable for active data processing, while non-volatile
     memory provides permanent storage for data that must be retained even when the device is off.
     idrdxYhla f,i" kYH u;lh fõ.j;a iy ;djld,sl fõ" il%Sh o;a; ieliSu i|yd iqÿiq fõ" kYH

(b)(i) A student asks you how all applications started by him execute simultaneously although he has a
       single-processor computer. Write down your explanation.
       ;udg ;ks ilikhla (single processor) iys; mß.Klhla ;snqko" ;ud úiska tys wrUk ish¨uefhÿï
       tljr l%shd;aul jkafka flfiaoehs YsIHfhla Tfnka úuihs' Tfí meyeÈ,s lsÍu ,shd olajkak'

     When a student runs multiple applications on a single-processor computer, it may seem like they are
     executing simultaneously. However, the computer can only execute one instruction at a time due to its
     single processor. The illusion of simultaneous execution is achieved through a process called context
     switching.
     YsIHfhl= ;ks ilikhla iys; mß.Klhl fhÿï lsysmhla Odjkh lrk úg" tAjd tljr l%shd;aul
     jk njla fmfkkakg mq¿jk' flfia fj;;a" mß.Klhg tys ;ks ilikh ksid tl jrlg l%shd;aul
     l< yelafla tla Wmfoia muKs' ikao¾Nh udre lsÍu kï l%shdj,shla yryd tljr l%shd;aul lsÍfï
     ñ;Hdj idlaId;a lr.kq ,efí'

     How Applications Execute Simultaneously
     fhÿï tljr l%shd;aul jk wdldrh

     •   Multiprogramming
         nyq jevigykalrKh
         Multiprogramming is the ability of an operating system to execute multiple programs concurrently.
         The operating system loads several programs into memory and switches between them, ensuring that
         the CPU is always busy processing some task. This technique increases the efficiency of resource use,
         as the CPU doesn't sit idle while one program waits for I/O operations to complete.
         nyq jevigykalrKh hkq fufyhqï moaO;shlg tljr jevigyka lsysmhla l%shd;aul lsÍug we;s
         yelshdjhs' fufyhqï moaO;sh jevigyka lsysmhla u;lhg mgjd tAjd w;r udre jk w;r" CPU
         iEu úgu hï ld¾hhla ieliSfï ld¾h nyq, nj iy;sl lrhs' tla jevigykla ^I$O& fufyhqï
         iïmQ¾K jk f;la n,d isák úg CPU ksIal%Shj fkdisàu ksid fuu ;dlaIKh iïm;a Ndú;fha
         ld¾hlaIu;dj jeä lrhs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -31-                                          #ictfromabc

    •   Timesharing
        ld, úNdckh
        Time-sharing extends the concept of multiprogramming by allowing multiple users to interact with
        the computer simultaneously. The operating system divides CPU time into small intervals (time slices)
        and rapidly switches between tasks, giving the illusion that each user has their own dedicated machine.
        This is particularly useful in environments where multiple users need to run programs concurrently
        on the same system.
        ld,h-úNdckh nyq l%uf,aLk ixl,amh È.= lrhs" nyq mßYS,lhskag tljr mß.Klh iuÕ
        wka;¾ l%shd lsÍug bv i,ihs' fufyhqï moaO;sh CPU ld,h l=vd ld, mr;rhka ^ld, fldgia&
        j,g fnod ld¾hhka w;r fõ.fhka udre jk w;r" iEu mßYS,lfhl=gu ;ukaf.au lemjQ
        hka;%hla we;s njg ñ;Hdj ,nd fohs' mßYS,lhka lsysm fofkl=g tlu moaO;shl tljr
        jevigyka l%shd;aul lsÍug wjYH mßirhkays§ fuh úfYaIfhka m%fhdackj;a fõ'

    •   Context Switching
        ikao¾Nh iaúph
        The operating system (OS) rapidly switches between different applications, saving the state of the
        currently running application and loading the state of the next one. This switching occurs so quickly
        that it appears to the user that all applications are running at the same time.
        fufyhqï moaO;sh (OS) úúO fhÿï w;r fõ.fhka udre jk w;r" oekg l%shd;aul jk fhÿfï
        ;;a;jha    iq/lSu iy B<Õ tflys ;;a;ajh mQrKh lrhs' fuu udreùu b;d blaukska isÿ jk
        w;r" ish¨u fhÿï tljr l%shd;aul jk nj mßYS,lhdg fmfka'

    •   Scheduling
        Wmf,aLk.; lsÍu
        The OS uses scheduling algorithms to determine which application to run at any given moment. This
        involves allocating CPU time to each application based on priority and resource needs.
        ´kEu fudfyd;l Odjkh l< hq;= fhÿu ;SrKh lsÍug OS kshulr lsÍfï we,af.dß;u Ndú;d
        lrhs' m%uqL;d iy iïm;a wjYH;d u; mokïj tla tla fhÿu i|yd CPU úNdckh lsrsu fuhg
        we;=<;a fõ'

    In summary, while a single-processor computer can only execute one task at a time, context switching,
    multiprogramming, and time-sharing techniques enable it to manage multiple applications effectively,
    creating the illusion of simultaneous execution.
    idrdxYhla f,i" ;ks ilikhla iys; mß.Klhlg tljr tla ld¾hhla muKla l%shd;aul l< yels
    w;r" ikao¾Nh iaúph " nyq l%uf,aLkh iy ld,h úNdckh lsrsfï Ys,amSh l%u uÕska nyq fhÿï
    M,odhs f,i l<ukdlrKh lsÍug yels jk w;r" tljr l%shd;aul lsÍfï ñ;Hdj ks¾udKh lrhs'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                              -32-                                         #ictfromabc

 (ii) Programs whose sizes are even larger than the size of the available physical memory of a computer
      could be executed on it. How can that be possible?
      mß.Klhl Ndú;hg .;yels fN!;sl u;lfha (physical memory) úYd,;ajhg jvd jeä
      úYd,;ajfhka hq;a l%uf,aL jqjo tu mß.Klfha Odjkh l< yel' tfia yels jkafka flfia o?

    Programs larger than the available physical memory of a computer can still be executed due to the
    implementation of virtual memory. This technique allows the operating system to use both physical
    memory (RAM) and secondary storage (like hard drives) to create an illusion of a larger memory space
    for running applications.
    w;:H u;lh l%shd;aul lsÍu fya;=fjka mß.Klhl mj;sk fN!;sl u;lhg jvd úYd, jevigyka
    ;ju;a l%shd;aul l< yel' fuu ;dlaIKh uÕska fufyhqï moaO;shg fN!;sl u;lh ^RAM& iy

    How Virtual Memory Works / w;:H u;lh l%shd lrk wdldrh
    •   Address Translation : The operating system maps virtual addresses used by programs to physical
        addresses in RAM. This mapping is managed by a component known as the Memory Management
        Unit (MMU).
        ,smsk mßj¾;kh ( fufyhqï moaO;sh RAM ys fN!;sl ,smsk fj; jevigyka Ndú;d lrk w;:H
        ,smsk is;shï.; lrhs' fuu is;shïlrKh u;l l<ukdlrK tAllh ^MMU& f,i y÷kajk
        ixrplhla u.ska l<ukdlrKh flfra'
    •   Paging : Virtual memory divides the program into smaller units called pages. Only the necessary pages
        are loaded into physical memory when needed, while others remain on the disk. This on-demand
        loading is known as demand paging.
        msgqlrKh ( w;:H u;lh uÕska jevigyk msgq f,i ye¢kafjk l=vd tAllj,g fnod we;' wjYH
        úg wjYH msgq muKla fN!;sl u;lhg mgjkq ,nk w;r wfkla tAjd ;eáfha mj;S' fuu b,a¨u
        u; megùu b,a¨u msgqlrKh f,i ye¢kafõ'
    •   Swapping : If the physical memory is full, the OS can swap out less frequently used pages to disk,
        freeing up space for the active pages of the currently running program. This allows the execution of
        programs larger than the available RAM.
        yqjudre lsÍu ( fN!;sl u;lh msÍ ;sfí kï" oekg l%shd;aul jk l%uf,aLfha il%sh msgq i|yd
        bv ksoyia lrñka" wvqfjka Ndú; jk msgq ;eáhg udre lsÍug OS yg yelsh' fuh mj;sk RAM
        m%udKhg jvd úYd, jevigyka l%shd;aul lsÍug bv i,ihs'

    Benefits of Virtual Memory / w;:H u;lfha m%;s,dN
    •   Increased Capacity : Virtual memory enables systems to run larger applications and multiple processes
        simultaneously, even when their combined sizes exceed physical memory.
        Odß;dj jeä lsÍu ( w:;H u;lh moaO;sj,g úYd, fhÿï iy nyq l%shdj,s tljr l%shd;aul
        lsÍug yelshdj ,nd fohs" tAjdfha tAldnoaO m%udK fN!;sl u;lh blaujk úg mjd'
    •   Efficient Resource Utilization : By loading only the required pages into memory, the system can
        manage resources more effectively, allowing for better multitasking and responsiveness.
        ld¾hlaIu iïm;a m%fhdackh( wjYH msgq muKla u;lhg mQrKh lsÍfuka" moaO;shg iïm;a jvd;a
        M,odhS f,i l<ukdlrKh l< yels w;r" jvd fyd| nyq ld¾h iy m%;spdr oelaùug bv i,ihs'
    •   Isolation and Security : Each process operates in its own virtual address space, which enhances
        security and stability by preventing processes from interfering with each other.
        yqol,d lsÍu iy wdrlaIdj ( iEu l%shdj,shlau ;ukaf.au w:;H ,smsk wjldYh ;=< l%shd;aul
        jk w;r tuÕska l%shdj,Ska tlsfkldg ndOd lsÍu je<elaùu u.ska wdrlaIdj iy ia:djr;ajh jeä lrhs'

    In summary, virtual memory allows programs larger than the physical memory to execute by using a
    combination of RAM and disk space, effectively managing memory through techniques like paging and
    swapping. This capability enhances system performance and user experience by enabling the execution of
    larger applications without the need for additional physical memory.
    idrdxYhla f,i" w;:H u;lh uÕska fN!;sl u;lhg jvd úYd, jevigyka l%shd;aul lsÍug RAM
    iy ;eá wjldYfha ixfhdackhla Ndú;d lsÍug bv i,ihs" msgqlrKh iy yqjudre lsÍu jeks
    ;dlaIKsl l%u yryd u;lh ld¾hlaIuj l<ukdlrKh lrhs' fuu yelshdj wu;r fN!;sl u;lhla
    wjYH fkdù úYd, fhÿï l%shd;aul lsÍu il%Sh lsÍu u.ska moaO;s l%shdldß;ajh iy mßYS,l w;aoelSï
    jeä ÈhqKq lrhs'

 (iii) When linked allocation is used for disk space allocation, each file needs slightly more storage space
       than when contiguous allocation is used. Explain the reason for it.
       äial bv mejÍu i|yd ine¢ úNckh (linked allocation) Ndú; lrk úg" iEu f.dkqjla i|ydu" hdno
       úNckh (contiguous allocation) Ndú; lsÍfï§ wjYHjk bv m%udKhg jvd hï;ñka jeä bv m%udKhla
       wjYH fõ' thg fya;=j myokak'

     When using linked allocation for disk space management, each file requires slightly more storage than
     when using contiguous allocation. This is primarily due to the overhead introduced by the pointers that
     link the blocks of a file together in linked allocation.
     ineÈ únckh i|yd iïnkaê; fjkalsÍï Ndú;d lrk úg" hdno únckh Ndú;d lrk úg jvd
     ;rula jeä .nvdjla wjYH fõ' fuhg uQ,sl jYfhka fya;= ù we;af;a ineÈ únckh§ f.dkqjl
     fldgia tlg iïnkaO lrk fmdhskag¾ u.ska y÷kajd ÿka Wäia fyâ tlhs'

     Reasons for Increased Storage Requirement in Linked Allocation
     ineÈ únckh§ .nvd wjYH;djh jeä ùug fya;=

     Pointers for Linking / ine¢ lsÍu i|yd o¾Yl :
      In linked allocation, each block of a file contains a pointer to the next block in the sequence. This pointer
     is necessary for the operating system to traverse the linked list of blocks to access the file's data. For
     example, if each pointer requires 4 bytes, and a file consists of multiple blocks, the total space used for
     pointers can accumulate significantly, especially for larger files.
     ineÈ únckh§ f.dkqjl iEu fldgilu wkqms<sfjf,ys B<Õ fldgi fj; o¾Ylhla wvx.= fõ'
     f.dkqfõ o;a; fj; m%fõY ùu i|yd iïnkaê; fldaI ,ehsia;=j yryd hdug fufyhqï moaO;shg fuu
     o¾Ylh wjYH fõ' WodyrKhla f,i" tla tla fmdhskagrhg nhsÜ 4la wjYH kï iy f.dkqjla fldaI
     lsysmhlska iukaú; kï" fmdhskag¾ i|yd Ndú;d lrk uq¿ bv m%udKh ie,lsh hq;= f,i tl;= úh
     yel" úfYaIfhka úYd, f.dkq i|yd'

     Non-contiguous Storage / fkdne¢ wdphkh :
     Unlike contiguous allocation, where a file occupies a single, continuous block of space, linked allocation
     allows blocks to be scattered throughout the disk. This flexibility requires additional metadata (the
     pointers) to keep track of the locations of each block, which contributes to the overall storage overhead.
     hdno únckh fuka fkdj" f.dkqjla ;ks" wLKav bv m%udKhla .kakd úg" iïnkaê; fjka lsÍu uÕska
     l=Üá ;eáh mqrd úisÍ hdug bv i,ihs' fuu kuHYS,SNdjhg tla tla fldgfiys ia:dk ksÍlaIKh
     lsÍug wu;r mdro;a; ^fmdhskag¾& wjYH jk w;r th iuia; .nvd fmdÿ ld¾h i|yd odhl fõ'

     Access Efficiency / m%fõY ld¾hlaIu;dj :
     The need to follow pointers during file access can lead to additional disk reads, which can slow down
     performance. However, this is more about access efficiency than storage space. The storage overhead
     remains a factor due to the pointers themselves.
     f.dkq m%fõYh w;r;=r o¾Yl wkq.ukh lsÍfï wjYH;djh wu;r ;eá lshùï j,g ;=vq Èh yels
     w;r tuÕska ld¾h idOkh ukao.dó úh yel' flfia fj;;a" fuh .nvd bv m%fõYhg jvd m%fõY
     ld¾hlaIu;dj .ek jeä h' o¾Yl ksid .nvd Wäia idOlhla f,i mj;S'

     In contrast, contiguous allocation only requires the starting block address and the total number of blocks
     to manage a file, leading to more efficient use of space since no additional pointers are necessary. Thus,
     linked allocation, while flexible and preventing fragmentation, incurs extra storage costs due to the need
     for pointers in each block.
     hdno únckh i|yd wjYH jkafka f.dkqjla l<ukdlrKh lsÍu i|yd wdrïNl fldaI ,smskh iy
     uq¿ fldaI ixLHdj muKla jk w;r" wu;r fmdhskag¾ wjYH fkdjk neúka wjldYh jvd;a
     ld¾hlaIuj Ndú;d lsÍug u. mdohs' fï wkqj" ineÈ únckh" kuHYS,S iy LKavkh ùu j<lajk
     w;ru" tla tla fldgfiys o¾Yl wjYH;djh fya;=fjka wu;r .nvd msßjehla orhs'

    For Extra Knowledge / wu;r oekqu i|yd

    1. Contiguous Allocation / hdno úNckh
       In contiguous allocation, each file is stored in a sequence of contiguous blocks on the disk. This means
       that all the blocks belonging to a file are stored next to each other.
       hdno úNckfh§" iEu f.dkqjlau ;eáfha tl È.g fldaI wkqms<sfj,lg .nvd lr we;' fuhska
       woyia lrkafka f.dkqjlg wh;a ish¨u fldaI tl <Õ .nvd lr we;s njhs'

          Advantages/ jdis        Disadvantages/ wjdis
       Fast Access           Fragmentation
       fõ.j;a m%fõY          LKavkh
       Simple Implementation File Size Limitation
       ir, l%shd;aul lsÍu f.dkq m%udKh iSud lsÍu

    2. Linked Allocation / ineÈ úNckh
       In linked allocation, each file is stored as a linked list of disk blocks. Each block contains a pointer to
       the next block in the file, rather than storing the file in contiguous blocks.
       ineÈ úNckfha§" iEu f.dkqjlau ;eá fldaI iïnkaê; ,ehsia;=jla f,i .nvd lr we;' iEu
       fldaIhlau f.dkqj tl <Õ ;sfnk fldaIj, .nvd lrkjdg jvd" f.dkqfõ B<Õ fldgi fj;
       o¾Ylhla wvx.= fõ'

         Advantages/ jdis  Disadvantages/ wjdis
       No Fragmentation    Slower Access
       LKavkh ke;          ukao.dó m%fõYh
       Dynamic File Size   Reliability.
       .;sl f.dkq úYd,;ajh úYajikSh;ajh'

    3. Chained Allocation / odu úNckh
       Chained allocation is similar to linked allocation but with a slight variation. In chained allocation,
       multiple files or file segments can be chained together through pointers. Each file segment can be
       stored in any available block, and the last block of one file or segment can point to the first block of
       another file or segment.
       odu úNckh iïnkaê; m%;smdokhg iudk kuq;a iq¿ fjkila we;' odu úNckfha§" nyq f.dkq
       fyda f.dkq fldgia fmdhskag¾ yryd tlg ne¢h yel' iEu f.dkq LKavhlau mj;sk ´kEu fldaIhl
       .nvd l< yels w;r" tla f.dkqjl fyda fldgil wjidk fldgi fjk;a f.dkqjl fyda fldgil
       m<uq fldgi fj; fhduq l< yel'

                Advantages / jdis                 Disadvantages / wjdis
       Flexibility                             Complexity
       kuHYS,S nj                              ixlS¾K;ajh
       Efficient Use of Space                  Slower Access
       wjldYh ld¾hlaIuj Ndú;d lsÍu             ukao.dó m%fõYh
